Conformal removability of SLE 4 {\rm SLE}_{4}
Konstantinos Kavvadias, Jason Miller, Lukas Schoug

Abstract
. We consider the Schramm-Loewner evolution ( SLE κ {\rm SLE}_{\kappa} ) with κ = 4 \kappa=4 , the critical value of κ > 0 \kappa>0 at or below which SLE κ {\rm SLE}_{\kappa} is a simple curve and above which it is self-intersecting. We show that the range of an SLE 4 {\rm SLE}_{4} curve is a.s. conformally removable, answering a question posed by Sheffield. Such curves arise as the conformal welding of a pair of independent critical ( γ = 2 \gamma=2 ) Liouville quantum gravity (LQG) surfaces along their boundaries and our result implies that this conformal welding is unique. In order to establish this result, we give a new sufficient condition for a set X ⊆ 𝐂 X\subseteq\mathbf{C} to be conformally removable which applies in the case that X X is not necessarily the boundary of a simply connected domain.



1.
Introduction

The Schramm-Loewner evolution (
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
) is a one parameter family (
\kappa>0
) of curves which connect two boundary points of a simply connected domain. It was introduced by Schramm in
[
48
]
as a candidate to describe the scaling limit of the interfaces which arise in discrete models from statistical mechanics on planar lattices at criticality, such as loop-erased random walk and the percolation model. Scaling limit results for such models towards
{\rm SLE}
have now been proved in a number of cases
[
55
,
29
,
49
,
56
]
and similar results have also been proved where the underlying graph is a random planar map
[
54
,
23
,
12
,
32
,
13
]
. The value of
\kappa>0
determines the roughness of an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
curve. In particular, the
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
curves are simple if
\kappa\leq 4
, self-intersecting but not space-filling if
\kappa\in(4,8)
, and space-filling if
\kappa\geq 8

[
47
]
. Moreover, the dimension of an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
curve is
\min(1+\kappa/8,2)

[
47
,
5
]
. The focus of this work is on the critical value
\kappa=4
, which corresponds to a curve which is simple but just barely so.

In recent years, there has been a substantial amount of work centered around the relationship between
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
and Liouville quantum gravity (LQG) surfaces. Roughly speaking, LQG is the canonical model of a random two-dimensional Riemannian manifold and can formally be described by its metric tensor

(1.1)

e^{\gamma h(z)}(dx^{2}+dy^{2})

where
h
is an instance of (some form of) the Gaussian free field (GFF) on a planar domain
D
and
\gamma\in(0,2]
is a parameter. Since the GFF is a distribution in the sense of Schwartz, it is non-trivial to make sense of (
1.1
) as the exponential is a non-linear operation and this has been the focus of a tremendous amount of mathematical work in the last two decades. For example, Kahane’s theory of Gaussian multiplicative chaos
[
20
]
can be used to construct the volume form and boundary length measure associated with (
1.1
). The work
[
10
]
gives another construction of the volume form and boundary length measure. Moreover,
[
10
]
proves a rigorous version of the KPZ formula, which was previously used by physicists to give non-rigorous derivations of critical exponents for planar lattice models (and more recently)
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
. At the critical value
\gamma=2
, the volume form and boundary length measure were constructed in
[
8
,
9
]
and later shown to arise as properly renormalized limits of the volume form and boundary length measure as
\gamma\to 2
in
[
3
]
. The metric (two point distance function) associated with (
1.1
) for
\gamma=\sqrt{8/3}
was constructed in
[
38
,
39
]
and for
\gamma\in(0,2)
in
[
6
,
14
]
.

The manner in which
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
arises in the context of LQG is that it describes the interfaces which are formed when one takes two independently sampled LQG surfaces and glues them together along their boundaries. The way that the gluing is constructed is using conformal welding, which we recall is defined as follows. Suppose that
\mathbf{D}_{1}
,
\mathbf{D}_{2}
are two copies of the unit disk and
\phi\colon\partial\mathbf{D}_{1}\to\partial\mathbf{D}_{2}
is a homeomorphism. We say that a simple loop
\eta
on the two-dimensional sphere
\mathbf{S}^{2}
together with conformal maps
\psi_{i}
for
i=1,2
from
\mathbf{D}_{i}
to the left and right sides of
\mathbf{S}^{2}\setminus\eta
is a conformal welding with welding homeomorphism
\phi
if
\phi=\psi_{2}^{-1}\circ\psi_{1}|_{\partial\mathbf{D}_{1}}
. For a given homeomorphism
\phi
, it is not obvious if such a conformal welding exists but Sheffield
[
53
]
showed that for each
\gamma\in(0,2)
it does exist if one takes the welding homeomorphism to be the one which associates points along the boundaries of two independent
\gamma
-LQG surfaces according to boundary length using the boundary measure from (
1.1
). In this case, the welding interface is an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
with
\kappa=\gamma^{2}\in(0,4)
. This was extended to the case
\kappa=4
and
\gamma=2
in
[
17
]
and we also remark that a number of welding-type results which include the case
\kappa>4
were established in
[
7
]
. (Let us also mention the work
[
4
]
which studies the conformal welding of an LQG surface to a Euclidean surface. In this case, curves different from
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
arise.)

For a homeomorphism
\phi
as above for which there exists a conformal welding, it is also a non-trivial question to determine whether the conformal welding is unique. Recall that a set
X\subseteq\mathbf{C}
is said to be
conformally removable
if every homeomorphism
\varphi\colon\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{C}
which is conformal on
\mathbf{C}\setminus X
is conformal on
\mathbf{C}
. It is not difficult to see that the uniqueness of a conformal welding is equivalent to the welding interface being conformally removable.

In order to prove that a set
X\subseteq\mathbf{C}
which is equal to the boundary of a simply connected domain
D
(e.g., a simple curve) is conformally removable, one often makes use of a condition due to Jones and Smirnov
[
19
]
. We will not describe the Jones-Smirnov condition here, but remark that one often checks that it holds by using one of the sufficient conditions established in
[
19
]
. For example, it is shown in
[
19
]
that if the Riemann map
\mathbf{D}\to D
is Hölder continuous up to
\partial\mathbf{D}
(so that
D
is a so-called Hölder domain) then
X=\partial D
is conformally removable; in fact it is shown in
[
19
]
that a much weaker modulus of continuity suffices (see also
[
24
]
). Rohde and Schramm proved that the complementary components of an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
curve for
\kappa\neq 4
are Hölder domains hence conformally removable. The optimal Hölder exponent was later computed in
[
15
]
in which it was also shown that
{\rm SLE}_{4}
does not form the boundary of a Hölder domain. The precise modulus of continuity of the uniformizing map of the complementary component of an
{\rm SLE}_{4}
was determined in
[
22
]
and is given by
(\log\delta^{-1})^{-1/3+o(1)}
as
\delta\to 0
. The reason for the difference in behavior for
\kappa\neq 4
is that
{\rm SLE}_{4}
curves are barely non-self-intersecting and contain tight bottlenecks. Another way of formulating this is that if
\eta
is an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
for
\kappa\in(0,4)
and
z\in\eta
then the harmonic measure of
B(z,\epsilon)
in the complement of
\eta
decays as fast as a power of
\epsilon
as
\epsilon\to 0

[
15
]
but for
\kappa=4
can decay as fast as
\exp(-\epsilon^{-3+o(1)})
as
\epsilon\to 0

[
22
]
. It was further shown in
[
22
]
that the Jones-Smirnov condition itself does not hold for
{\rm SLE}_{4}
.

The main result of this paper is the conformal removability of
{\rm SLE}_{4}
, which implies the uniqueness of the welding problem for critical (
\gamma=2
) LQG. (We remark that a weaker version of the uniqueness of the welding problem for critical LQG was proved in
[
33
]
.)

Theorem 1.1
.

Suppose that
\eta
is an
{\rm SLE}_{4}
in
\mathbf{H}
from
0
to
\infty
. Suppose that
f\colon\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{H}
is a homeomorphism which is conformal on
\mathbf{H}\setminus\eta
. Then
f
is a.s. conformal on
\eta
. In particular, the range of
\eta
is a.s. conformally removable.

We note that the first assertion of Theorem
1.1
implies that the range of
\eta
is a.s. conformally removable because if
f\colon\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{C}
is a homeomorphism which is conformal on
\mathbf{C}\setminus\eta
then by the Riemann mapping theorem we can post-compose its restriction to
\mathbf{H}
with a conformal map so that we obtain a homeomorphism
\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{H}
which is conformal on
\mathbf{H}\setminus\eta
. We also remark that Theorem
1.1
applies if
\eta
is an
{\rm SLE}_{4}
in an arbitrary simply connected domain
D
since one can always conformally map
D
to
\mathbf{H}
.

One of the main steps in proving Theorem
1.1
is Theorem
8.1
, which contains a new sufficient condition for a set
X\subseteq\mathbf{C}
to be conformally removable. One of the novelties of Theorem
8.1
is that it does not require
X=\partial D
for
D\subseteq\mathbf{C}
simply connected as in
[
19
]
. We also remark that in general the conformal removability for boundaries of domains which are not simply connected is less well-understood. Perhaps the simplest example is the standard Sierpinski carpet which (without much difficulty) is seen to not be conformally removable; see, e.g., the discussion after
[
42
, Theorem 1.8]
. See also the recent work
[
43
]
which proves that all topological Sierpinski carpets are not conformally removable. It is a much more difficult problem to show that the Sierpinski gasket is not conformally removable
[
42
]
. Like the Sierpinski gasket, the complement of an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
curve with
\kappa\in(4,8)
consists of a countable collection of open sets whose boundaries can intersect each other. In another work
[
21
]
, we will use Theorem
8.1
to prove the conformal removability of
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
for
\kappa\in(4,8)
whenever the adjacency graph of connected components of the complement of such a curve is connected. This means that if
\eta
is an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
in
\mathbf{H}
from
0
to
\infty
and
U,V
are components of
\mathbf{H}\setminus\eta
then there exist components
U_{1},\ldots,U_{n}
of
\mathbf{H}\setminus\eta
with
U_{1}=U
,
U_{n}=V
, and
\partial U_{i}\cap\partial U_{i+1}\neq\emptyset
for each
1\leq i\leq n-1
. We note that it was shown in
[
16
]
that there exists
\kappa_{0}\in(4,8)
so that this condition holds for all
\kappa\in(4,\kappa_{0})
.

We refer the reader to Theorem
8.1
for the precise statement of the sufficient condition for conformal removability, but we will give a rough description of the setup and strategy here. Suppose that
X\subseteq\mathbf{C}
has zero Lebesgue measure and
f\colon\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{C}
is a homeomorphism which is conformal on
\mathbf{C}\setminus X
. To show that
f
is conformal on
\mathbf{C}
it suffices to show that
f
is absolutely continuous on lines (ACL), which we recall means that it is absolutely continuous on Lebesgue a.e. horizontal and vertical line. To prove that
X
is conformally removable, it therefore suffices to control the variation of
f
on the places where a Lebesgue typical horizontal or vertical line intersects
X
. We will fix
a>0
small,
M>1
large, and assume that
X
has upper Minkowski dimension at most
2-5a
. We will further assume that we have a family of sets
\mathcal{A}=\cup_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{A}_{n}
where each
A\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
has the topology of an annulus with
\mathrm{diam}(A)\leq M2^{-n}
such that for each
z\in X
and
n\in\mathbf{N}
there exists
(1-a^{2})n\leq k\leq n
and
A\in\mathcal{A}_{k}
so that
B(z,2^{-n})
is contained in the bounded component of
\mathbf{C}\setminus A
and the following additional condition holds. There are finitely many open, simply connected, and pairwise disjoint sets
U_{1},\ldots,U_{m}
for
1\leq m\leq M
in
A\setminus X
with
\partial U_{i}\cap\partial U_{i+1}\neq\emptyset
for
1\leq i\leq m
(with
U_{m+1}=U_{1}
) which satisfy some additional assumptions on the geometry of their pairwise intersections. This will allow us to construct a path
\gamma
which disconnects the inner and outer boundaries of
A
and whose image under
f
has diameter at most
2^{-(1-3a)k}+2^{(1-a)k}\int_{A}|f^{\prime}(w)|^{2}d\mathcal{L}_{2}(w)
(where
\mathcal{L}_{2}
denotes two-dimensional Lebesgue measure). This gives an upper bound on
\mathrm{diam}(f([z-2^{-k},z+2^{-k}]))
which suffices because for (a compact part of) a typical line
L
the number of intervals of length
2^{-n}
hit by
X
is
O(2^{(1-5a)n})
(as
X
has upper Minkowski dimension at most
2-5a
) and the integral of
|f^{\prime}|^{2}
on the
2^{-(1-a^{2})n}
-neighborhood of (a compact part of)
L
is
O(2^{-(1-a^{2})n})
. In particular, the variation of
f
on (a compact part of)
L
in
X\cap L
if
O(2^{-an/2})
which tends to
0
as
n\to\infty
, hence
f
is absolutely continuous on
L
.

Outline and strategy

The remainder of this article is structured as follows. First, in Section
2
we will collect a number of preliminaries. Next, in Section
3
we will prove two regularity estimates for
{\rm SLE}_{4}
. We will next in Section
4
prove several estimates regarding the regularity of the first crossing of an
{\rm SLE}_{4}
across an annulus. The purpose of Section
5
is to define and study the properties of an exploration of crossings of GFF level lines across a rectangle
[
50
,
57
]
. We will then extend this to the case of annuli in Section
6
. We next show in Section
7
that the hypotheses of Theorem
8.1
are satisfied for
{\rm SLE}_{4}
. Finally, we will state and prove the removability theorem in Section
8
. Appendix
A
contains a number of facts regarding the relationship between Whitney squares and the hyperbolic metric.

Notation

For two quantities
a,b
we write
a\lesssim b
if there is a constant
C>0
(referred to as the implicit constant), independent of any parameters of interest, such that
a\leq Cb
. Moreover, we write
a\gtrsim b
if
b\lesssim a
, and
a\asymp b
if
a\lesssim b
and
a\gtrsim b
. We often specify what parameters the implicit constant depends on.

For a domain
D
,
E\subseteq\partial D
and
z\in D
, we denote by
\omega(z,E,D)
the harmonic measure of
E
in
D
seen from
z
. Moreover, for a given curve
\eta
we denote by
\eta^{\mathrm{L}}
(resp.
\eta^{\mathrm{R}}
) the left (resp. right) side of the curve in the sense of prime ends.

We let
\mathbf{Z}
denote the set of integers,
\mathbf{N}
the set of positive integers,
\mathbf{N}_{0}=\mathbf{N}\cup\{0\}
,
\mathbf{R}
the real numbers,
\mathbf{C}
the complex plane,
\mathbf{D}
the unit disk and
\mathbf{H}
the upper half-plane, and
\mathscr{S}=\mathbf{R}\times(0,\pi)
the infinite horizontal strip of height
\pi
. For a set
A\subseteq\mathbf{R}^{n}
, we let
\mathrm{cl}(A)
denote its closure. Finally, we denote by
\mathcal{L}_{n}
the
n
-dimensional Lebesgue measure.

2.
Preliminaries

We will now collect a number of preliminaries which are used throughout this work. We begin by reviewing some basic facts about Whitney squares and the hyperbolic metric in Section
2.1
, quasiconformal maps in Section
2.2
,
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
and its relevant variants in Section
2.3
, and the natural parameterization of
{\rm SLE}
in Section
2.4
. We next review the GFF in Section
2.5
, LQG in Section
2.6
, and finally the level line coupling of
{\rm SLE}_{4}
and its variants with the GFF in Section
2.7
.

2.1.
Whitney squares, hyperbolic and quasihyperbolic distance

Recall that the
hyperbolic metric
in
\mathbf{D}
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{hyp}}^{\mathbf{D}}(z_{1},z_{2})=\inf\int_{z_{1}}^{z_{2}}\frac{|dz|}{1-|z|^{2}}\quad\text{for}\quad z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbf{D},

where the infimum is taken over all smooth curves in
\mathbf{D}
connecting
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
. One of the basic properties of
\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{hyp}}^{\mathbf{D}}
is that it is conformally invariant which means that for each conformal automorphism
\varphi
of
\mathbf{D}
we have that
\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{hyp}}^{\mathbf{D}}(\varphi(z_{1}),\varphi(z_{2}))=\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{hyp}}^{\mathbf{D}}(z_{1},z_{2})
(see
[
11
, Chapter I.4]
). This makes it possible to define the hyperbolic metric on any simply connected domain
D\subseteq\mathbf{C}
by
\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{hyp}}^{D}(z_{1},z_{2})\coloneqq\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{hyp}}^{\mathbf{D}}(\varphi(z_{1}),\varphi(z_{2}))
where
\varphi:D\to\mathbf{D}
is a conformal map as
\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{hyp}}^{D}
does not depend on the choice of
\varphi
. It follows that the hyperbolic distance is conformally invariant which means that if
D,\widetilde{D}\subseteq\mathbf{C}
are simply connected domains,
\varphi:D\to\widetilde{D}
is a conformal map, and
z_{1},z_{2}\in D
, then we have that
\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{hyp}}^{D}(z_{1},z_{2})=\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{hyp}}^{\widetilde{D}}(\varphi(z_{1}),\varphi(z_{2}))
.

We note that for each
z\in\mathbf{D}
,
\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{hyp}}^{\mathbf{D}}(z,\partial\mathbf{D})=\infty
and hence the same holds in any simply connected domain
D
. However, the notion of a geodesic between an interior point and a boundary point still makes sense. We define the hyperbolic geodesic from
0
to
1
in
\mathbf{D}
to be the line segment
[0,1]
. The reason for this definition is that for each
\epsilon\in(0,1)
the curve with the shortest hyperbolic length connecting
0
and
\partial B(1,\epsilon)\cap\mathbf{D}
is the segment
[0,1-\epsilon]
. Moreover, for a Jordan domain
D
,
z\in D
,
w\in\partial D
, we define the hyperbolic geodesic from
z
to
w
to be
\varphi([0,1])
, where
\varphi:\mathbf{D}\to D
is the unique conformal map such that
\varphi(0)=z
and
\varphi(1)=w
. This definition makes sense more generally if
D\subseteq\mathbf{C}
is a simply connected domain,
z\in D
, and
w
is a prime end of
\partial D
. Throughout the paper, for
z,w\in\mathrm{cl}(D)
, we shall denote by
\gamma_{z,w}^{D}
the hyperbolic geodesic from
z
to
w
in
D
, parameterized so that the hyperbolic length of
\gamma_{z,w}^{D}([s,t])
is
t-s
, and extended to all times
t\in\mathbf{R}
.

For a simply connected domain
D\subseteq\mathbf{C}
one can also consider the
quasihyperbolic metric

\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{qh}}^{D}
which is defined by

\displaystyle\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{qh}}^{D}(z_{1},z_{2})=\inf\int_{z_{1}}^{z_{2}}\frac{|dz|}{\mathrm{dist}(z,\partial D)}\quad\text{for}\quad z_{1},z_{2}\in D,

where the infimum is taken over all smooth curves in
D
connecting
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
. It follows by the Koebe-
1/4
theorem that for any simply connected domain
D
we have

(2.1)

\displaystyle\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{hyp}}^{D}(z,w)\leq\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{qh}}^{D}(z,w)\leq 4\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{hyp}}^{D}(z,w);

see
[
11
, Chapter I.4]
.

For any open subset
U\subsetneq\mathbf{C}
, there exists a family
\mathcal{W}=(Q_{j})
of closed squares with pairwise disjoint interiors and sides parallel to the axes, such that
Q_{j}
has sidelength
{\mathrm{len}}(Q_{j})=2^{-n_{j}}
for some
n_{j}\in\mathbf{Z}
,
U=\cup_{j}Q_{j}
and such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{diam}(Q_{j})\leq\mathrm{dist}(Q_{j},\partial U)<4\mathrm{diam}(Q_{j}).

Such a family is referred to as a
Whitney square
decomposition of
U
and we let
\mathrm{cen}{Q_{j}}
denote the center of
Q_{j}\in\mathcal{W}
. In what follows, we assume that
U=D
is a simply connected domain. Consider a Whitney square decomposition
\mathcal{W}=(Q_{j})
of
D
and write
x_{j}=\mathrm{cen}(Q_{j})
. Furthermore, let
G_{\mathcal{W}}=(V_{\mathcal{W}},E_{\mathcal{W}})
denote the graph with vertex set
V=(x_{j})
and edge set
E
such that
\{x_{i},x_{j}\}\in E
if and only if
\partial Q_{i}\cap\partial Q_{j}
contains an open interval. Denote by
d_{\mathcal{W}}
the graph distance in
G_{\mathcal{W}}
, that is,
d_{\mathcal{W}}(x_{i},x_{j})
is the minimal number of edges of a path in
G_{\mathcal{W}}
from
x_{i}
to
x_{j}
. In other words,
d_{\mathcal{W}}(x_{i},x_{j})
is the minimal number of adjacent Whitney squares one needs to traverse to travel from
x_{i}
to
x_{j}
. Then it follows that

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{W}}(x_{i},x_{j})\asymp\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{qh}}(x_{i},x_{j})

where the implicit constants are universal. Furthermore, we write
x(z)=x_{j}
if and only if
z\in Q_{j}
and we denote by
Q_{j}^{\dagger}
the square with center
x_{j}
such that
{\mathrm{len}}(Q_{j}^{\dagger})={\mathrm{len}}(Q_{j})/2
. Then, noting that if
z,w\in\mathrm{cl}(Q_{j})
for some
j
, then
\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{qh}}^{D}(z,w)\leq 1
and
\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{qh}}^{D}(z,x_{j})\leq 1/2
, it follows that

(2.2)

\displaystyle\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{qh}}^{D}(z,w)\asymp d_{\mathcal{W}}(x(z),x(w))

whenever
w\in Q_{j}^{\dagger}
and
z\in Q_{i}
,
i\neq j
, where the implicit constants are universal. By (
2.1
), we also have that

(2.3)

\displaystyle\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{hyp}}^{D}(z,w)\asymp d_{\mathcal{W}}(x(z),x(w))

holds for some universal implicit constants.

We wrap up this subsection with a few useful inequalities for Whitney square decompositions and conformal maps.

We note that if
\mathcal{W}
is a Whitney square decomposition and
Q,Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}
are adjacent then there exists a constant
M>1
such that

(2.4)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{M}{\mathrm{len}}(Q^{\prime})\leq{\mathrm{len}}(Q)\leq M{\mathrm{len}}(Q^{\prime}).

Let
D
and
\widetilde{D}
be simply connected domains,
f:D\to\widetilde{D}
be a conformal transformation, and let
\mathcal{W}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}
be Whitney square decompositions of
D
and
\widetilde{D}
, respectively. By
[
26
, Theorem 3.21]
there exist universal constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that for all
Q\in\mathcal{W}
and
z\in Q
we have

(2.5)

\displaystyle c_{1}|f^{\prime}(\mathrm{cen}(Q))|\leq|f^{\prime}(z)|\leq c_{2}|f^{\prime}(\mathrm{cen}(Q))|.

Moreover, by the Koebe-
1/4
theorem, letting
Q(z)
(resp.
\widetilde{Q}(f(z))
) denote any square
Q\in\mathcal{W}
(resp.
\widetilde{Q}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}
) such that
z\in\mathrm{cl}(Q)
(resp.
f(z)\in\mathrm{cl}(\widetilde{Q})
), we have that

(2.6)

\displaystyle|f^{\prime}(z)|\asymp\frac{\mathrm{dist}(f(z),\partial\widetilde{D})}{\mathrm{dist}(z,\partial D)}\asymp\frac{\mathrm{diam}(\widetilde{Q}(f(z)))}{\mathrm{diam}(Q(z))}=\frac{{\mathrm{len}}(\widetilde{Q}(f(z)))}{{\mathrm{len}}(Q(z))},

where the implicit constants are universal.

2.2.
Quasiconformal maps

Let
D,\widetilde{D}
be domains in
\widehat{\mathbf{C}}=\mathbf{C}\cup\{\infty\}
and let
f:D\to\widetilde{D}
be an orientation preserving homeomorphism. We say that
f
is
ACL
(absolutely continuous on lines) if
f
is absolutely continuous on Lebesgue a.e. line segment in
D
which is parallel to one of the axes. For
M\geq 1
, we say that
f
is an
M
-quasiconformal mapping if
f
is ACL and

(2.7)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\partial f}{\partial\overline{z}}\right|\leq\frac{M-1}{M+1}\left|\frac{\partial f}{\partial z}\right|\quad\text{a.e.}

(with the understanding that
\partial f/\partial z
and
\partial f/\partial\overline{z}
are defined a.e.). The smallest constant
M\geq 1
such that (
2.7
) holds is called the dilation of
f
on
D
. The dilation is the maximal factor with which infinitesimal circles can be dilated or extremal distance can increase or decrease, see
[
11
, Chapter VII.3]
, when transformed by
f
. We say that
f
is quasiconformal if it is
M
-quasiconformal for some
M\geq 1
. Moreover, it holds that
f
is a conformal map if and only if
f
is a
1
-quasiconformal map. For more material on quasiconformal maps as well as other equivalent definitions, see for example
[
1
,
11
,
44
]
.

The central notion in this paper will be that of (quasi)conformal removability. We say that a set
K\subseteq D
is
(quasi)conformally removable inside a domain

D
if any homeomorphism
f
which is (quasi)conformal on
D\setminus K
is (quasi)conformal on
D
. One can apply the measurable Riemann mapping theorem (see
[
1
]
) to see that any planar set is quasiconformally removable if and only if it is conformally removable.

To show that a set
K\subseteq D
of zero (two-dimensional) Lebesgue measure is conformally removable in
D
, then it suffices to show that every homeomorphism
f
on
D
which is conformal on
D\setminus K
is ACL. Indeed, the fact that
f
is conformal on
D\setminus K
implies that (
2.7
) holds a.e. with
M=1
. If in addition,
f
is ACL, then
f
is
1
-quasiconformal and hence conformal.

2.3.
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
and
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\underline{\rho})
processes

2.3.1.
Chordal
{\rm SLE}

As we mentioned in the introduction,
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
is a one-parameter family of conformally invariant random curves indexed by
\kappa>0
introduced by Schramm in
[
48
]
as a candidate for the scaling limit of the interfaces in discrete lattice models in two dimensions at criticality.
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
in
\mathbf{H}
from
0
to
\infty
is defined in terms of the family of conformal maps
(g_{t})
which are obtained by solving the chordal Loewner equation

(2.8)

\partial_{t}g_{t}(z)=\frac{2}{g_{t}(z)-W_{t}},\quad g_{0}(z)=z\in\mathbf{H}

with
W=\sqrt{\kappa}B
and
B
a standard Brownian motion. We write
K_{t}=\{z\in\mathbf{H}:\tau_{z}\leq t\}
where
\tau_{z}=\sup\{t\geq 0:\mathrm{Im}(g_{t}(z))>0\}
. Then
g_{t}
is the unique conformal transformation from
\mathbf{H}_{t}=\mathbf{H}\setminus K_{t}
onto
\mathbf{H}
satisfying
\lim_{z\to\infty}|g_{t}(z)-z|=0
. It is shown in
[
47
]
for
\kappa\neq 8
that the family of hulls
(K_{t})
is generated by a continuous curve which means there exists a curve
\eta\colon[0,\infty)\to\mathrm{cl}(\mathbf{H})
so that
\mathbf{H}_{t}
is equal to the unbounded component of
\mathbf{H}\setminus\eta([0,t])
for each
t\geq 0
. The analogous result for
\kappa=8
was proved in
[
29
]
as a consequence of the convergence of the uniform spanning tree Peano curve to
{\rm SLE}_{8}
(see also
[
2
]
for another proof which does not use discrete models).

An
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
connecting boundary points
x
and
y
of an arbitrary simply connected domain
D
is defined as the image of an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
in
\mathbf{H}
from
0
to
\infty
under a conformal transformation
\varphi:\mathbf{H}\rightarrow D
sending
0
to
x
and
\infty
to
y
. Moreover, it was shown in
[
47
]
that
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
is a.s. a simple curve for
\kappa\in(0,4]
intersecting the domain boundary only at its starting and ending points while for
\kappa\geq 8
the curve is a.s. space-filling. For
\kappa\in(4,8)
,
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
intersects itself and the domain boundary but is not space-filling.

The
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\underline{\rho})
processes are variants of
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
in which one keeps track of extra marked points
[
25
, Section 8.3]
called force points. Suppose that we have vectors
\underline{\rho}^{L}=(\rho^{\ell,L},\ldots,\rho^{1,L})
and
\underline{\rho}^{R}=(\rho^{1,R},\ldots,\rho^{r,R})
(which give the weights of the force points to the left and right of
0
, respectively) and
\underline{x}^{L}=(x^{\ell,L}<\ldots<x^{1,L}\leq 0)
and
\underline{x}^{R}=(0\leq x^{1,R}<\ldots<x^{r,R})
(which give the locations of the force points to the left and right of
0
). Then an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\underline{\rho})={\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\underline{\rho}^{L};\underline{\rho}^{R})
process is defined in the same way as ordinary
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
except with
W
taken to solve

\displaystyle dW_{t}=\sqrt{\kappa}dB_{t}+\sum_{q\in\{L,R\}}\sum_{i}\frac{\rho^{i,q}dt}{W_{t}-V_{t}^{i,q}},\quad dV_{t}^{i,q}=\frac{2dt}{V_{t}^{i,q}-W_{t}},\quad V_{0}^{i,q}=x_{i}^{q},\quad\text{for}\quad q\in\{L,R\}.

It was shown in
[
35
]
that there is a unique solution to this SDE up to the first
t
such that
\sum_{i:V_{t}^{i,L}=W_{t}}\rho^{i,L}\leq-2
or
\sum_{i:V_{t}^{i,R}=W_{t}}\rho^{i,R}\leq-2
. This time
t
is the so-called
continuation threshold
. Moreover, it was shown in
[
35
]
that the
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\underline{\rho})
processes are generated by a continuous curve up to the continuation threshold. The continuity of the (single force point)
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\rho)
processes with
\rho\in(-2-\kappa/2,-2)
was proved in
[
40
,
37
]
. This gives the full range of
\rho
values in which an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\rho)
process can be defined.

2.3.2.
Radial and whole-plane
{\rm SLE}

We will also need to consider the radial and whole-plane
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\rho)
processes. They are defined in terms of the radial form of the Loewner equation

(2.9)

\partial_{t}g_{t}(z)=g_{t}(z)\frac{W_{t}+g_{t}(z)}{W_{t}-g_{t}(z)},\quad g_{0}(z)=z

where
W\colon[0,\infty)\to\partial\mathbf{D}
is a continuous function. In the case of radial
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
, one takes
W=e^{i\sqrt{\kappa}B}
where
B
is a standard Brownian motion. Let

\Psi(z,w)=-z\frac{z+w}{z-w}\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{\Psi}(z,w)=\frac{\Psi(z,w)+\Psi(1/\overline{z},w)}{2}.

Fix
\rho\in\mathbf{R}
. For radial
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\rho)
, one instead takes
W
to be the solution to the SDE

(2.10)

dW_{t}=\left(-\frac{\kappa}{2}W_{t}+\frac{\rho}{2}\widetilde{\Psi}(O_{t},W_{t})\right)dt+i\sqrt{\kappa}W_{t}dB_{t},\quad dO_{t}=\Psi(W_{t},O_{t})dt.

By a comparison to a Bessel process, the SDE (
2.10
) has a solution which is defined for all
t\geq 0
provided
\rho>-2
. For each
z\in\mathbf{D}
we let
\tau_{z}=\sup\{t\geq 0:|g_{t}(z)|<1\}
and
K_{t}=\{z\in\mathbf{D}:\tau_{z}\leq t\}
. Then
g_{t}
is the unique conformal transformation
\mathbf{D}\setminus K_{t}\to\mathbf{D}
with
g_{t}(0)=0
and
g_{t}^{\prime}(0)>0
. In fact, due to the normalization in (
2.9
) one has that
g_{t}^{\prime}(0)=e^{t}
for each
t\geq 0
.

Whole-plane
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
and
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\rho)
are defined by solving (
2.9
) for all
t\in\mathbf{R}
where in the former case
W=e^{i\sqrt{\kappa}B_{t}}
for a two-sided Brownian motion
B
and in the latter case
W
is taken to be a stationary solution to (
2.10
) defined for all
t\in\mathbf{R}
. In this case, for each
t\in\mathbf{R}
we have that
g_{t}
is the unique conformal transformation from
\mathbf{C}\setminus K_{t}
to
\mathbf{C}\setminus\mathrm{cl}(\mathbf{D})
which fixes and has positive derivative at
\infty
and
(K_{t})
is an increasing family of compact hulls in
\mathbf{C}
starting from
0
. That radial and whole-plane
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\rho)
processes correspond to a continuous curve for
\rho>-2
follows from the continuity of the chordal
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(\rho)
processes (see
[
36
, Section 2]
).

2.3.3.
Two-sided whole-plane
{\rm SLE}

Finally, we will also consider whole-plane two-sided
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
from
\infty
to
\infty
through
0
. This path can be constructed by first sampling a whole-plane
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}(2)
process
\eta_{1}
from
0
to
\infty
, then a chordal
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
process
\eta_{2}
in
\mathbf{C}\setminus\eta_{1}
from
0
to
\infty
, and then taking the concatenation of the time-reversal of
\eta_{1}
together with
\eta_{2}
. Roughly speaking, this process describes the local behavior of an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
near an interior point
z
when it is conditioned to pass through
z
.

2.4.
Natural parameterization for
{\rm SLE}

Implicit in the definition of
{\rm SLE}
in the chordal, radial, and whole-plane cases respectively using (
2.8
) and (
2.9
) is the so-called capacity time parameterization. This time parameterization is natural from the perspective of the Loewner equation in its chordal and radial form. Another time parameterization for
{\rm SLE}
is the so-called
natural time parameterization
, which is conjectured to be the time parameterization which arises when considering
{\rm SLE}
as the scaling limit of an interface of a discrete model in which the curve is parameterized by the number of edges it traverses. An indirect construction of the natural parameterization of
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
was given first for
\kappa<4(7-\sqrt{33})
in
[
30
]
and then for all
\kappa<8
in
[
31
]
. In the case that
\kappa\geq 8
, the natural parameterization corresponds to parameterizing the curve according to the amount of Lebesgue measure it fills (this is the natural definition in this case as such
{\rm SLE}
curves are space-filling). In
[
28
]
, a direct construction was given, where the authors proved that the natural parameterization of
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
is in fact equal to (a constant times) the
d_{\kappa}=(1+\kappa/8)
-dimensional Minkowski content of
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
(which they proved a.s. exists). Any curve
\eta
whose law is locally absolutely continuous with respect to the law of an
{\rm SLE}
process has a corresponding natural parameterization, which we will henceforth denote by
\mu_{\eta}^{\mathrm{nat}}
. We view
\mu_{\eta}^{\mathrm{nat}}
as measure on the Borel
\sigma
-algebra of subsets of
\mathbf{C}
where
\mu_{\eta}^{\mathrm{nat}}(A)=\mathcal{L}_{1}(\eta^{-1}(A))
when
\eta
has the natural parameterization.

One of the important properties of the natural parameterization is that it is
conformally covariant
. This means that if
\eta
is an
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
-type curve in
D
,
\varphi\colon D\to\widetilde{D}
is a conformal map, and
\widetilde{\eta}=\varphi(\eta)
then
\mu_{\widetilde{\eta}}^{\mathrm{nat}}(\varphi(A))=\int_{A}|\varphi^{\prime}(z)|^{d_{\kappa}}d\mu_{\eta}^{\mathrm{nat}}(z)
for every Borel set
A\subseteq\mathbf{C}
.

Let us now recall the definition of Minkowski content. Consider a set
S\subseteq\mathbf{C}
and
d\in(0,2)
. For
r>0
we set

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cont}_{d}(S;r)

\displaystyle=e^{r(2-d)}\mathcal{L}_{2}(\{z:\mathrm{dist}(z,S)\leq e^{-r}\})=e^{r(2-d)}\int_{\mathbf{C}}{\bf 1}_{\{\mathrm{dist}(z,S)\leq e^{-r}\}}d\mathcal{L}_{2}(z).

The
d
-dimensional Minkowski content of
S
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cont}_{d}(S)=\lim_{r\to\infty}\mathrm{Cont}_{d}(S;r),

provided that the limit exists.

Suppose that
\eta
is a two-sided whole-plane
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
process from
\infty
to
\infty
through
0
with the natural parameterization with time normalized so that
\eta(0)=0
. It was proved in
[
58
]
that the law of
\eta
is shift invariant in the sense that for each
t\in\mathbf{R}
the law of
\eta^{t}=\eta(\cdot+t)-\eta(t)
has the same law as
\eta
. This fact will be important for us in Section
3
.

2.5.
Gaussian free fields

Let
D\subseteq\mathbf{C}
be a simply connected domain with harmonically non-trivial boundary and let
H_{0}(D)
be the Hilbert space closure of
C_{0}^{\infty}(D)
with respect to the Dirichlet inner product
(f,g)_{\nabla}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{D}\nabla f(z)\cdot\nabla g(z)d\mathcal{L}_{2}(z)
. Then the zero boundary Gaussian free field (GFF)
h
is the random distribution defined by

(2.11)

h=\sum_{n\geq 1}\alpha_{n}\phi_{n},

where
(\phi_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is an orthonormal basis of
H_{0}(D)
with respect to
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\nabla}
and
(\alpha_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is a sequence of i.i.d.
N(0,1)
random variables. The convergence in (
2.11
) holds a.s. in
H^{-\epsilon}(D)
for each
\epsilon>0

[
52
]
.

Other variants of the GFF are defined using a series expansion as in (
2.11
). For example, the free boundary GFF is defined in the same way except that we replace
H_{0}(D)
by the closure
H(D)
with respect to
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\nabla}
of the space of functions
f\in C^{\infty}(D)
with
\|f\|_{\nabla}<\infty
such that
\int_{D}f(z)d\mathcal{L}_{2}(z)=0
. Note that in this way, the free boundary GFF is defined in the space of distributions modulo additive constant. However, we can fix the additive constant by fixing the value of the field when acting on a given test function whose mean is non-zero. Also, the whole-plane GFF is defined in the same way as the free boundary GFF, but with the orthonormal basis in (
2.11
) being that of the Hilbert space closure with respect to
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\nabla}
of the set of
f\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbf{C})
satisfying
\int_{\mathbf{C}}f(z)d\mathcal{L}_{2}(z)=0
. Then the field is defined modulo additive constant but we can fix its additive constant as described above.

2.6.
Liouville quantum gravity

2.6.1.
LQG measure and surfaces

Fix
\gamma\in(0,2]
. As mentioned in Section
1
, a
\gamma
-LQG surface is formally a random two-dimensional Riemannian manifold with metric tensor

e^{\gamma h(z)}(dx^{2}+dy^{2})

where
dx^{2}+dy^{2}
is the Euclidean metric,
h
is some form of the GFF on a planar domain
D
, and
\gamma\in(0,2]
. Since
h
is a distribution and not a function, this expression requires interpretation. For
\gamma\in(0,2)
, the associated
\gamma
-LQG area measure with respect to
h
on
D
can be defined as

(2.12)

\mu_{h}(dz)=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon^{\gamma^{2}/2}e^{\gamma h_{\epsilon}(z)}d\mathcal{L}_{2}(z),

where
h_{\epsilon}(z)
denotes the average of
h
on
\partial B(z,\epsilon)
for
0<\epsilon<\mathrm{dist}(z,\partial D)
(see
[
10
]
). In order to obtain a non-trivial limit in the case
\gamma=2
, one instead considers
[
8
,
9
]

(2.13)

\mu_{h}(dz)=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}(\log\epsilon^{-1})^{1/2}\epsilon^{2}e^{2h_{\epsilon}(z)}d\mathcal{L}_{2}(z).

In the case that
h
is (some form of) a GFF with free boundary conditions, the
\gamma
-LQG boundary length measure is defined in an analogous way. We note that it is also possible to define the measure for
\gamma=2
as a renormalized limit as
\gamma\uparrow 2
of the LQG measure for
\gamma\in(0,2)

[
3
]
.

Suppose that
h
is an instance of (some form of) the GFF on a domain
D\subseteq\mathbf{C}
and
\mu_{h}
is as above. Suppose that
\widetilde{D}\subseteq\mathbf{C}
is another domain,
\psi:\widetilde{D}\to D
is a conformal map, and set

(2.14)

\widetilde{h}=h\circ\psi+Q\log|\psi^{\prime}|\quad\text{where}\quad Q=\frac{2}{\gamma}+\frac{\gamma}{2}.

Then we have that
\mu_{\widetilde{h}}(A)=\mu_{h}(\psi(A))
for all
A\subseteq D
Borel. This leads to the definition of a
quantum surface
, which is an equivalence class of pairs
(D,h)
, where
D\subseteq\mathbf{C}
is a domain and
h\in H^{-1}(D)
is a random distribution on
D
, where two such pairs are equivalent if there exists a conformal map
\psi\colon\widetilde{D}\to D
so that
h
,
\widetilde{h}
are related as in (
2.14
). We can also consider quantum surfaces
(D,h,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
,
(\widetilde{D},\widetilde{h},\widetilde{x}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{x}_{n})
with marked points. In this case, they are considered to be equivalent if they satisfy (
2.14
) and
\psi(\widetilde{x}_{j})=x_{j}
for all
j=1,\ldots,n
.

2.6.2.
Quantum wedges and cones

We now proceed to give the definition of a
quantum wedge
and a
quantum cone
, which are the two types of quantum surfaces which will be relevant for this work. The former (resp. latter) is a doubly marked surface which is naturally parameterized by either
\mathbf{H}
or the infinite strip
\mathscr{S}=\mathbf{R}\times(0,\pi)
(resp.
\mathbf{C}
or the infinite cylinder
\mathscr{C}=\mathbf{R}\times[0,2\pi)
, with the top and bottom identified). Quantum wedges and cones are each in fact one parameter families of quantum surfaces. One convenient choice of parameterization is the
weight
because for wedges it is compatible with the welding operation. In this paper, we will only consider the case
\gamma=2
and the case of weight
\tfrac{\gamma^{2}}{2}=2
quantum wedges and weight
4
quantum cones.

Definition 2.1
.

A
quantum wedge of weight
\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}
is the doubly marked quantum surface
(\mathscr{S},h,-\infty,+\infty)
whose law can be sampled from as follows. Let
X_{t}
for
t\in\mathbf{R}
be the process which for
t\leq 0
is equal to
-X_{-2t}^{1}
where
X^{1}
has the law of a
\mathrm{BES}^{3}
and for
t\geq 0
is equal to
X_{2t}^{2}
where
X^{2}
is a standard Brownian motion where
X_{0}^{1}=X_{0}^{2}=0
and
X^{1},X^{2}
are independent. Then for each
t\in\mathbf{R}
the average of
h
on the vertical line
\{t\}\times(0,\pi)
is equal to
X_{t}
and the law of
h_{2}=h-X_{\mathrm{Re}(\cdot)}
is that of the projection of a free boundary GFF onto the space of functions in
H(\mathscr{S})
which have mean zero on vertical lines where
X
,
h_{2}
are taken to be independent.

We note that embeddings of a quantum surface parameterized by
\mathscr{S}
with marked points at
-\infty
,
+\infty
differ only by a horizontal translation. The particular choice of embedding in Definition
2.1
is the so-called
circle average embedding
of a quantum wedge. Applying the conformal map
\mathscr{S}\to\mathbf{H}
given by
z\mapsto\exp(z)
gives the law of a quantum wedge parameterized by
\mathbf{H}
instead of
\mathscr{S}
.

Definition 2.2
.

Fix
W>0
. A
quantum cone of weight
W
is the doubly marked quantum surface
(\mathscr{C},h,-\infty,+\infty)
whose law can be sampled from as follows. Let
a=W/(2\gamma)
and let
X_{t}:\mathbf{R}\to\mathbf{R}
be the process
B_{t}+at
where
B_{t}
is a standard two-sided Brownian motion conditioned such that
B_{t}+at>0
for all
t>0
. Then for each
t\in\mathbf{R}
the average of
h
on the vertical line
\{t\}\times(0,2\pi)
is equal to
X_{t}
and the law of
h_{2}=h-X_{\mathrm{Re}(\cdot)}
is that of the projection of a whole-plane GFF onto the space of functions in
H(\mathscr{C})
which have mean zero on vertical lines where
X
,
h_{2}
are taken to be independent.

We note that embeddings of a quantum surface parameterized by
\mathscr{C}
with marked points at
-\infty
,
+\infty
differ only by a horizontal translation. The particular choice of embedding in Definition
2.2
is the so-called
circle average embedding
of a quantum cone. Applying the conformal map
\mathscr{C}\to\mathbf{C}
given by
z\mapsto\exp(z)
gives the law of a quantum cone parameterized by
\mathbf{C}
instead of
\mathscr{C}
.

A number of results regarding the cutting and welding of quantum wedges and cones were proved in
[
53
,
7
]
for
\gamma\in(0,2)
and for
\gamma=2
in
[
17
]
. In this work, we will need to make use of an extension of one of the results in
[
7
]
to the case
\gamma=2
which was proved in
[
22
]
.

Theorem 2.3
.

Suppose that
(\mathbf{C},h,0,\infty)
is a weight
4
quantum cone and
\eta
is a two-sided whole-plane
{\rm SLE}_{4}
from
\infty
to
\infty
through
0
. Then the quantum surfaces parameterized by the two components of
\mathbf{C}\setminus\eta
and marked by
0
and
\infty
are independent quantum wedges of weight
2
.

2.7.
Level lines

Let
\lambda=\pi/2
and suppose that
h
is an instance of the GFF on
\mathbf{H}
with boundary conditions given by
-\lambda
(resp.
\lambda
) on
\mathbf{R}_{-}
(resp.
\mathbf{R}_{+}
). Even though
h
is a distribution and not a function, it is shown in
[
50
]
that it is possible to make sense of a zero level line
\eta
of
h
from
0
to
\infty
and its law is that of a chordal
{\rm SLE}_{4}
. Moreover,
\eta
is a path-valued function of
h
which is characterized by the property that for each
t\geq 0
the conditional law of
h
given
\eta|_{[0,t]}
is that of a GFF in
\mathbf{H}\setminus\eta([0,t])
with boundary conditions given by
-\lambda
(resp.
\lambda
) on the left (resp. right) side of
\eta([0,t])
and
\mathbf{R}_{-}
(resp.
\mathbf{R}_{+}
).

In
[
57
]
, the methods introduced in
[
50
]
were generalized to the setting in which the GFF has piecewise constant boundary data which changes values at most finitely many times. More precisely, suppose that we have fixed force points
(\underline{x}^{L};\underline{x}^{R})
and weights
(\underline{\rho}^{L};\underline{\rho}^{R})
as in the definition of
{\rm SLE}_{4}(\underline{\rho}^{L};\underline{\rho}^{R})
. Let
h
be a GFF on
\mathbf{H}
with boundary conditions given by
-\lambda(1+\sum_{i=0}^{j}\rho^{i,L})
for
x\in[x^{j+1,L},x^{j,L})
and
\lambda(1+\sum_{i=0}^{j}\rho^{i,R})
for
x\in[x^{j,R},x^{j+1,R})
where
\rho^{0,L}=\rho^{0,R}=0
,
x^{0,L}=0^{-}
,
x^{\ell+1,L}=-\infty
,
x^{0,R}=0^{+}
, and
x^{r+1,R}=\infty
. Then the zero level line of
h
from
0
to
\infty
has the law of an
{\rm SLE}_{4}(\underline{\rho}^{L};\underline{\rho}^{R})
in
\mathbf{H}
from
0
to
\infty
. As above,
\eta
is a path valued function of the field which is characterized by the property that for each
t\geq 0
the conditional law of
h
given
\eta|_{[0,t]}
is that of a GFF in
\mathbf{H}\setminus\eta([0,t])
with boundary conditions given by
-\lambda
(resp.
\lambda
) on the left (resp. right) side of
\eta([0,t])
and the same boundary values as
h
on
\partial\mathbf{H}
.

When
(h,\eta)
are as above, we refer to
\eta
as the height
0
level line of
h
from
0
to
\infty
. More generally, for each
u\in\mathbf{R}
the height
u
level line of
h
is given by the
0
height level line of
h-u
from
0
to
\infty
. Note that then the boundary data for
h-u
along such a level line is
-\lambda
(resp.
\lambda
) on its left (resp. right) side hence the boundary data for
h
is given by
u-\lambda
(resp.
u+\lambda
) on its left (resp. right) side. The results of
[
57
]
moreover describe the interaction of GFF level lines starting from different points and serves to generalize the flow line interaction rules established in
[
35
]
. (We note that, in
[
57
]
, they call the level line of
h+u
the height
u
level line, rather than the height
-u
level line and hence the following interaction result is mirrored, compared to
[
57
]
.)

Theorem 2.4
.

Suppose that
h
is a GFF on
\mathbf{H}
whose boundary value is piecewise constant. For each
u\in\mathbf{R}
and each
x\in\partial\mathbf{H}
, we let
\eta_{u}^{x}
be the level line of
h
with height
u
starting from
x
. Fix
x_{1}\leq x_{2}
.

(1)

If
u_{2}>u_{1}
then
\eta_{u_{2}}^{x_{2}}
a.s. stays to the right of
\eta_{u_{1}}^{x_{1}}
.

(2)

If
u_{2}=u_{1}
then
\eta_{u_{2}}^{x_{2}}
may intersect
\eta_{u_{1}}^{x_{1}}
at the boundary and, upon intersecting, the two curves merge and do not subsequently separate.

(3)

If
u_{2}-u_{1}\geq 2\lambda
then
\eta_{u_{1}}^{x_{1}}
,
\eta_{u_{2}}^{x_{2}}
a.s. do not intersect (except at possibly their starting point).

Finally in
[
57
]
, the authors proved the following about the reversibility of the level lines of the GFF.

Theorem 2.5
.

Suppose that
h
is a GFF on
\mathbf{H}
whose boundary value is piecewise constant. Let
\eta
be the level line of
h
starting from
0
targeted at
\infty
and let
\widetilde{\eta}
be the level line of
-h
starting from
\infty
targeted at
0
. Then, on the event that the two paths do not hit the continuation threshold before they reach the target points, the two paths
\widetilde{\eta}
and
\eta
are equal (viewed as sets) a.s.

3.
Estimates for
{\rm SLE}_{4}

This section is devoted to the proof of two regularity estimates for
{\rm SLE}_{4}
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Suppose that we have a two-sided whole-plane
{\rm SLE}_{4}
process
\eta^{w}
which has the natural parameterization and is normalized so that
\eta^{w}(0)=0
. For each
a,\delta,p\in(0,1)
there exists
C>0
so that the following is true. Let
V
be the component of
\mathbf{C}\setminus\eta^{w}
which contains
1
, let
\mathcal{W}
be a Whitney square decomposition of
V
, and let
Q_{0}\in\mathcal{W}
be the square which contains
1
. Let
\mathcal{T}
be the set of times
t\in[0,1]
so that the hyperbolic geodesic from
1
to
\eta^{w}(t)
in
V
satisfies the property that for every
Q\in\mathcal{W}
which it hits we have that
\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{hyp}}^{V}(\mathrm{cen}(Q),1)\leq C{\mathrm{len}}(Q)^{-a}
. Then
\mathbf{P}[\mathcal{L}_{1}(\mathcal{T})\geq 1-\delta]\geq p
.

Proof.

Step 1. Setup.
We assume that we have the notation and setup described in the lemma statement. We also let
U
be uniform in
[0,1]
independently of
\eta^{w}
and let
(\mathbf{C},h,0,\infty)
be a quantum cone of weight
4
with the circle average embedding which is independent of
(\eta^{w},U)
. We then consider the translation map
\psi_{U}\colon\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{C}
given by
z\mapsto z+\eta^{w}(U)
and set
h^{U}=h\circ\psi_{U}^{-1}
(note that
(\psi_{U}^{-1})^{\prime}\equiv 1
). Then the quantum surface
(\mathbf{C},h^{U},\eta^{w}(U),\infty)
is a quantum cone of weight
4
with the circle average embedding since the quantum surface
(\mathbf{C},h,0,\infty)
has the law of a quantum cone of weight
4
with the circle average embedding and it is independent of
(\eta^{w},U)
. We also let
V_{1}
(resp.
V_{2}
) be the connected component of
\mathbf{C}\setminus\eta^{w}
lying to the right (resp. left) side of
\eta^{w}
, and for
j=1,2
we consider the conformal transformation
\phi_{U}^{j}:V_{j}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
such that
\phi_{U}^{j}(\eta^{w}(U))=-\infty
,
\phi_{U}^{j}(\infty)=+\infty
and set
\widetilde{h}^{U,j}=h^{U}\circ(\phi_{U}^{j})^{-1}+Q\log|((\phi_{U}^{j})^{-1})^{\prime}|
. We pick the third parameter in the definition of
\phi_{U}^{j}
so that the quantum surface
(\mathscr{S},\widetilde{h}^{U,j},-\infty,+\infty)
has the circle average embedding. Since
(\eta^{w}(s+U)-\eta^{w}(U))_{s\in\mathbf{R}}
has the same law as
\eta^{w}
, it follows from
[
22
, Theorem 3.1]
that the surfaces

(\mathscr{S},\widetilde{h}^{U,1}-\infty,+\infty)
and
(\mathscr{S},\widetilde{h}^{U,2},-\infty,+\infty)
are independent and they both have the law of a quantum wedge of weight
2
parameterized by
\mathscr{S}
with the circle average embedding. Note that
\widetilde{h}^{U,j}
can be decomposed as
\widetilde{h}^{U,j}=h_{1}^{j}+h_{2}^{j}
where
h_{1}^{j}\in\mathcal{H}_{1}(\mathscr{S})
and
h_{2}^{j}\in\mathcal{H}_{2}(\mathscr{S})
. We will abuse notation and write
h_{1}^{j}(t)
for the common value of
h_{1}^{j}
on the vertical segment
\{t\}\times(0,\pi)
. We also have that
(h_{1}^{j}(t))_{t\leq 0}
(resp.
(h_{1}^{j}(t))_{t\geq 0}
) has the same law as
(-X^{j}_{-2t})_{t\leq 0}
(resp.
(B^{j}_{2t})_{t\geq 0}
) where
X^{j}
(resp.
B^{j}
) is a
\mathrm{BES}^{3}
process (resp. standard Brownian motion) starting from
0
and
X^{j}
and
B^{j}
are independent of each other. Moreover
h_{2}^{j}
is independent of
h_{1}^{j}
and has the same law as the corresponding projection to
\mathcal{H}_{2}(\mathscr{S})
of a free boundary GFF on
\mathscr{S}
.

Step 2. Initial estimates.
Next we fix
q\in(0,1)
small (to be chosen and depending only on
a
,
\delta
, and
p
). Let
V_{j_{0}}
for
j_{0}\in\{1,2\}
be the connected component of
\mathbf{C}\setminus\eta^{w}
containing
1
. Since
\phi_{U}^{j_{0}}(1)\in\mathscr{S}
, we can find
R\in\mathbf{N}
and
r\in(0,\pi/2)
so that with
K=[-R,R]\times[r,\pi-r]
we have that
\mathbf{P}[\phi_{U}^{j_{0}}(1)\in K]\geq 1-q/100
. For
k\in\mathbf{N}_{0}
, we consider the rectangles
W_{k}^{-}=[-k-1,-k]\times[r,\pi-r]
and
W_{k}^{+}=[k,k+1]\times[r,\pi-r]
. Let
z_{k}^{-}
(resp.
z_{k}^{+}
) be the center of
W_{k}^{-}
(resp.
W_{k}^{+}
). Let also
\phi\coloneqq(\phi_{U}^{j_{0}})^{-1}
. Note that
[
26
, Theorem 3.21]
implies that there exist finite universal constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
depending only on
r
such that

(3.1)

c_{1}|\phi^{\prime}(z_{k}^{-})|\leq|\phi^{\prime}(z)|\leq c_{2}|\phi^{\prime}(z_{k}^{-})|\quad\text{for all}\quad z\in W_{k}^{-}\quad\text{and}\quad k\in\mathbf{N}_{0}

and a similar statement holds with
z_{k}^{-}
and
W_{k}^{-}
replaced by
z_{k}^{+}
and
W_{k}^{+}
. We also claim that we can find finite constants
c_{3},c_{4}>0
depending only on
r
such that for all
k\in\mathbf{N}_{0}
we have

(3.2)

c_{3}|\phi^{\prime}(z_{k}^{-})|\leq{\mathrm{len}}(Q)\leq c_{4}|\phi^{\prime}(z_{k}^{-})|\quad\text{for}\quad Q\in\mathcal{W},\ Q\cap\phi(W_{k}^{-})\neq\emptyset

and a similar statement holds if
Q\cap\phi(W_{k}^{+})\neq\emptyset
when we replace
z_{k}^{-}
and
W_{k}^{-}
by
z_{k}^{+}
and
W_{k}^{+}
. Indeed, let
Q\in\mathcal{W}
be such that
Q\cap\phi(W_{k}^{-})\neq\emptyset
and fix
w\in Q\cap\phi(W_{k}^{-})
. Then by the Koebe-1/4 theorem and (
3.1
) we have that

\displaystyle\mathrm{dist}(Q,\partial V_{j_{0}})\leq\mathrm{dist}(w,\partial V_{j_{0}})\leq 4c_{2}r|\phi^{\prime}(z_{k}^{-})|.

Moreover,
\sqrt{2}{\mathrm{len}}(Q)=\mathrm{diam}(Q)\leq\mathrm{dist}(Q,\partial V_{j_{0}})
and thus
{\mathrm{len}}(Q)\leq 2\sqrt{2}c_{2}r|\phi^{\prime}(z_{k}^{-})|
. On the other hand, we have that

\displaystyle\mathrm{dist}(w,\partial V_{j_{0}})\leq\mathrm{dist}(Q,\partial V_{j_{0}})+\mathrm{diam}(Q)\leq 5\mathrm{diam}(Q)=5\sqrt{2}{\mathrm{len}}(Q)

and by the Koebe-1/4 theorem,

\displaystyle\mathrm{dist}(w,\partial V_{j_{0}})\geq\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{dist}(\phi_{U}^{j_{0}}(w),\partial\mathscr{S})|\phi^{\prime}(\phi_{U}^{j_{0}}(w))|\geq\frac{c_{1}r}{4}|\phi^{\prime}(z_{k}^{-})|.

Consequently,
{\mathrm{len}}(Q)\asymp|\phi^{\prime}(z_{k}^{-})|
with implicit constants depending only on
r
. This proves (
3.2
) whenever
Q\cap\phi(W_{k}^{-})\neq\emptyset
and similarly whenever
Q\cap\phi(W_{k}^{+})\neq\emptyset
. Moreover, there exist finite constants
c_{5},c_{6}>0
depending only on
r
such that

(3.3)

c_{5}|\phi^{\prime}(z_{k}^{-})|\leq\mathrm{diam}(\phi(W_{k}^{-}))\leq c_{6}|\phi^{\prime}(z_{k}^{-})|

and similarly with
z_{k}^{-}
and
W_{k}^{-}
replaced by
z_{k}^{+}
and
W_{k}^{+}
. To see this, we first note that
[
26
, Corollary 3.25]
implies that there exists a finite constant
c_{*}>0
depending only on
r
such that
B(\phi(z_{k}^{-}),c_{*}|\phi^{\prime}(z_{k}^{-})|)\subseteq\phi(W_{k}^{-})
and so
\mathrm{diam}(\phi(W_{k}^{-}))\gtrsim|\phi^{\prime}(z_{k}^{-})|
. Also (
3.2
) implies that
\mathrm{diam}(\phi(W_{k}^{-}))\lesssim|\phi^{\prime}(z_{k}^{-})|
where the implicit constant depends only on
r
.

Step 3. Quantum area lower bounds.
Next, we focus on giving lower bounds on
\mu_{\widetilde{h}^{U,j}}(W_{k}^{\pm})
for
k\in\mathbf{N}_{0}
and
j=1,2
. By considering a fine grid of points in
B(\eta^{w}(U),1)
and applying
[
22
, Lemma 4.3]
it follows that there exist
\epsilon_{0}\in(0,1)
and
u>0
such that with probability at least
1-q/100
we have that

(3.4)

\mu_{h^{U}}(B(w,\epsilon))\geq\epsilon^{u}\quad\text{for all}\quad w\in B(\eta^{w}(U),1/2)\quad\text{and all}\quad\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0}].

Also, we have that
\mathrm{dist}(\eta^{w}(U),\phi(W_{k}^{-}))\to 0
as
k\to\infty
and so combining with (
3.3
) implies that there exists
k_{0}\in\mathbf{N}_{0}
such that with probability at least
1-q/100
we have that
c_{*}|\phi^{\prime}(z_{k}^{-})|\leq\epsilon_{0}
for all
k\geq k_{0}
. Moreover, since
\mu_{\widetilde{h}^{U,j}}(W_{k}^{-})>0
and
\mu_{\widetilde{h}^{U,j}}(W_{k}^{+})>0
for all
k\in\mathbf{N}_{0}
and
j=1,2
a.s., we can find a finite constant
C_{1}>0
such that with probability at least
1-q/100
we have that
|\phi^{\prime}(z_{k}^{-})|^{u}\leq C_{1}\mu_{\widetilde{h}^{U,j_{0}}}(W_{k}^{-})
for all
0\leq k\leq k_{0}
and
|\phi^{\prime}(z_{k}^{+})|^{u}\leq C_{1}\mu_{\widetilde{h}^{U,j_{0}}}(W_{k}^{+})
for all
0\leq k\leq R
. Combining, we obtain that we can find constants
u,C_{1}>0
such that with probability at least
1-q/3
we have that
\phi_{U}^{j_{0}}(1)\in K
,

\displaystyle|\phi^{\prime}(z_{k}^{-})|^{u}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\mu_{h^{U}}(B(\phi(z_{k}^{-}),c_{*}|\phi^{\prime}(z_{k}^{-})|))\leq C_{1}\mu_{h^{U}}(\phi(W_{k}^{-}))=C_{1}\mu_{\widetilde{h}^{U,j_{0}}}(W_{k}^{-})\quad\text{for all}\quad k\in\mathbf{N}_{0}

and

\displaystyle|\phi^{\prime}(z_{k}^{+})|^{u}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\mu_{h^{U}}(B(\phi(z_{k}^{+}),c_{*}|\phi^{\prime}(z_{k}^{+})|))\leq C_{1}\mu_{h^{U}}(\phi(W_{k}^{+}))=C_{1}\mu_{\widetilde{h}^{U,j_{0}}}(W_{k}^{+})\quad\text{for all}\quad 0\leq k\leq R.

Step 4. Quantum area upper bounds.
Now, we turn to give some high probability upper bounds on
\mu_{\widetilde{h}^{U,j}}(W_{k}^{\pm})
. We note that the ideas are similar to those of the proof of
[
22
, Lemma A.1]
. First, we observe that

\displaystyle\mu_{\widetilde{h}^{U,j}}(W_{k}^{-})

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(2\sup_{-k-1\leq t\leq-k}h_{1}^{j}(t)\right)\mu_{h_{2}^{j}}(W_{k}^{-})=\exp\left(-2\inf_{2k\leq t\leq 2(k+1)}X_{t}^{j}\right)\mu_{h_{2}^{j}}(W_{k}^{-})

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-2\inf_{2k\leq t\leq 2(k+1)}(X_{t}^{j}-X_{2k}^{j})\right)\exp(-2X_{2k}^{j})\mu_{h_{2}^{j}}(W_{k}^{-}).

By the Markov property of
X^{j}
it follows that
(X_{t}^{j}-X_{2k}^{j})_{t\geq 2k}
and
X_{2k}^{j}
are independent and that
-\inf_{2k\leq t\leq 2(k+1)}(X_{t}^{j}-X_{2k}^{j})
is stochastically dominated by
\widetilde{S}^{j}=\sup_{0\leq t\leq 2}\widetilde{B}_{t}^{j}
where
\widetilde{B}_{t}^{j}
is a standard Brownian motion with
\widetilde{B}_{0}^{j}=0
. Consequently, it follows by
[
22
, Lemma A.4]
that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\!\left[e^{2s\widetilde{S}^{j}}\mu_{h_{2}^{j}}(W_{k}^{-})^{s}\right]=\mathbf{E}\!\left[e^{2s\widetilde{S}^{j}}\mu_{h_{2}^{j}}(W_{0})^{s}\right]<\infty\quad\text{for each}\quad s\in(0,1/2).

Thus Markov’s inequality implies that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\!\left[e^{2\widetilde{S}^{j}}\mu_{h_{2}^{j}}(W_{k}^{-})\geq k^{2/s}\right]\lesssim k^{-2},

where the implicit constant is independent of
k
.

Next, to treat the term
e^{2X_{2k}^{j}}
, we first note that the transition density for
X^{j}
is given by
p_{t}(0,y)=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}t^{-3/2}y^{2}e^{-y^{2}/2t}

[
45
, Chapter XI]
. Fix
b\in(0,1/6)
. Since
X^{j}
satisfies Brownian scaling we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\!\left[X_{2k}^{j}\leq k^{b}/2\right]=\mathbf{P}\!\left[X_{1}^{j}\leq k^{b-1/2}/2^{3/2}\right]\lesssim k^{3(b-1/2)},

where the implicit constant is independent of
k
. Therefore we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\!\left[\mu_{\widetilde{h}^{U,j}}(W_{k}^{-})\geq k^{2/s}e^{-k^{b}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{P}\!\left[e^{2\widetilde{S}^{j}}\mu_{h_{2}^{j}}(W_{k}^{-})\geq k^{2/s}\right]+\mathbf{P}\!\left[X_{2k}^{j}\leq k^{b}/2\right]\lesssim k^{-2}+k^{3(b-1/2)}

and so as
3(b-1/2)<-1
we have that

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 1}\mathbf{P}\!\left[\mu_{\widetilde{h}^{U,j}}(W_{k}^{-})\geq k^{2/s}e^{-k^{b}}\right]<\infty.

Thus we can find
k_{0}\in\mathbf{N}
such that with probability at least
1-q/100
we have that
\mu_{\widetilde{h}^{U,j}}(W_{k}^{-})\leq k^{2/s}e^{-k^{b}}
for all
k\geq k_{0}
and
j=1,2
. Again, by possibly decreasing
b>0
, there exists a constant
C_{2}>0
such that with probability at least
1-q/3
it holds that
\mu_{\widetilde{h}^{U,j}}(W_{k}^{-})\leq C_{2}e^{-k^{b}}
for all
k\in\mathbf{N}_{0}
and
\mu_{\widetilde{h}^{U,j}}(W_{k}^{+})\leq C_{2}e^{-k^{b}}
for all
0\leq k\leq R
and
j=1,2
.

Step 5. Conclusion of the proof.

Combining everything, we obtain that we can find constants
r,R,u,C_{1},C_{2}>0
and
b\in(0,1/6)
such that with probability at least
1-q
the following hold:

(i)

\phi_{U}^{j_{0}}(1)\in K
,

(ii)

|\phi^{\prime}(z_{k}^{-})|^{u}\leq C_{1}\mu_{\widetilde{h}^{U,j_{0}}}(W_{k}^{-})
for all
k\in\mathbf{N}_{0}
and
|\phi^{\prime}(z_{k}^{+})|^{u}\leq C_{1}\mu_{\widetilde{h}^{U,j_{0}}}(W_{k}^{+})
for all
0\leq k\leq R
, and

(iii)

\mu_{\widetilde{h}^{U,j}}(W_{k}^{-})\leq C_{2}e^{-k^{b}}
for all
k\in\mathbf{N}_{0}
and
\mu_{\widetilde{h}^{U,j}}(W_{k}^{+})\leq C_{2}e^{-k^{b}}
for all
0\leq k\leq R
and
j=1,2
.

From now on we assume that we are working on the event that (
i
), (
ii
) and (
iii
) hold.

Now we are ready to show that every
Q\in\mathcal{W}
which intersects the hyperbolic geodesic from
1
to
\eta^{w}(U)
satisfies
\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{hyp}}^{V_{j_{0}}}(\mathrm{cen}(Q),1)\leq C{\mathrm{len}}(Q)^{-a}
for some constant
C>0
. We set
z=\phi_{U}^{j_{0}}(1)\in K
. If
\mathrm{Im}(z)\neq\frac{\pi}{2}
, then
e^{z}\notin i\mathbf{R}
, and so the hyperbolic geodesic in
\mathbf{H}
between
e^{z}
and the origin is given by the arc in
\mathbf{H}
connecting
e^{z}
to the origin of the circle passing through
e^{z}
and the origin and whose center lies in
\mathbf{R}
. If
\mathrm{Im}(z)=\frac{\pi}{2}
, then
e^{z}\in i\mathbf{R}
and so the hyperbolic geodesic in
\mathbf{H}
between
e^{z}
and the origin is given by the segment in
i\mathbf{R}
connecting them. In either case, we let
\widetilde{\gamma}_{z}
be the image of the said geodesic under
w\mapsto\log(w)
. It follows that
\widetilde{\gamma}_{z}
is contained in
(-\infty,R]\times[r,\pi-r]
and that the image
\gamma_{U}
of
\widetilde{\gamma}_{z}
under
\phi
is the hyperbolic geodesic in
V_{j_{0}}
from
1
to
\eta^{w}(U)
.

From the definition of the hyperbolic metric it is not difficult to see that there exists a constant
C_{r}>0
depending only on
r
such that for all
k,m\in\mathbf{N}_{0}
,
\widetilde{z}\in W_{k}^{-}
and
\widetilde{w}\in W_{m}^{-}
, we have that
\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{hyp}}^{\mathscr{S}}(\widetilde{z},\widetilde{w})\leq C_{r}(1+|k-m|)
(e.g., by considering the hyperbolic length of the segment from
\widetilde{z}
to
\widetilde{w}
). The same also holds with the
W_{k}^{-}
’s replaced by the
W_{k}^{+}
’s. Let
Q
be a square in
\mathcal{W}
that
\gamma_{U}
intersects and fix
w\in Q\cap\gamma_{U}
. Then, either
\widetilde{z}=\phi_{U}^{j_{0}}(w)\in W_{k}^{-}
for some
k\in\mathbf{N}_{0}
or
\widetilde{z}\in W_{k}^{+}
for some
0\leq k\leq R
. Suppose that the former holds. Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{hyp}}^{V_{j_{0}}}(w,1)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{hyp}}^{\mathscr{S}}(\widetilde{z},z)\lesssim k.

Combining (
3.2
) with (
ii
) and (
iii
) we obtain that
e^{k^{b}}\lesssim{\mathrm{len}}(Q)^{-u}
. This implies that if we set
v=\frac{a}{u}
, then
k\lesssim{\mathrm{len}}(Q)^{-a}
since
k\lesssim e^{vk^{b}}
and so

\displaystyle\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{hyp}}^{V_{j_{0}}}(\mathrm{cen}(Q),1)\lesssim k\lesssim{\mathrm{len}}(Q)^{-a}

with the implicit constants depending only on
r
,
R
,
a
, and
q
. If
\widetilde{z}\in W_{k}^{+}
for some
0\leq k\leq R
, then a similar analysis holds and so
\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{hyp}}^{V_{j_{0}}}(\mathrm{cen}(Q),1)\leq C{\mathrm{len}}(Q)^{-a}
.

To finish the proof of the lemma, for
t\in[0,1]
we let
A_{t}
be the event that if
\gamma_{t}
is the hyperbolic geodesic in
V_{j_{0}}
connecting
1
to
\eta^{w}(t)
, then for every
Q
in
\mathcal{W}
such that
\gamma_{t}\cap Q\neq\emptyset
, we have that
\mathrm{dist}_{\mathrm{hyp}}^{V_{j_{0}}}(\mathrm{cen}(Q),1)\leq C{\mathrm{len}}(Q)^{-a}
. Then the analysis of the previous paragraphs implies that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}[\mathcal{L}_{1}(\{t\in[0,1]:A_{t}^{c}\text{ occurs}\})]\leq q.

Markov’s inequality therefore implies that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}[\mathcal{L}_{1}(\mathcal{T})<1-\delta]=\mathbf{P}[\mathcal{L}_{1}(\{t\in[0,1]:A_{t}^{c}\text{ occurs}\})\geq\delta]\leq\frac{q}{\delta}

and so the claim of the lemma follows by taking
q
to be sufficiently small.
∎

Lemma 3.2
.

Fix
\kappa\in(0,8)
and let
d_{\kappa}=1+\frac{\kappa}{8}
. Suppose that
\eta
is a chordal
{\rm SLE}_{\kappa}
in
\mathbf{H}
from
0
to
\infty
with the natural parameterization. For each compact set
K\subseteq\mathbf{H}
,
a>0
, and
p\in(0,1)
there exists
C>0
such that the following is true. With probability at least
p
for every Borel set
A\subseteq K
we have that
\mu_{\eta}^{\mathrm{nat}}(A)=\mathcal{L}_{1}(\eta^{-1}(A))\leq C\mathrm{diam}(A)^{d_{\kappa}-a}
.

Proof.

Let
R\subseteq\mathbf{H}
be a rectangle such that
K\subseteq R
and
d=\mathrm{dist}(K,\partial R)>0
. The proof follows closely that of
[
46
, Theorem 1.2]
. For a domain
D\subseteq\mathbf{H}
and
r>0
, we set

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cont}_{d_{\kappa}}(\eta\cap D;r)=r^{d_{\kappa}-2}\mathcal{L}_{2}(\{z\in D:\mathrm{dist}(z,\eta)\leq r\}).

Then it is shown in
[
28
]
that the limit

(3.5)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cont}_{d_{\kappa}}(\eta\cap D)=\lim_{r\to 0}\mathrm{Cont}_{d_{\kappa}}(\eta\cap D;r)

exists a.s. and it is the natural length of
\eta
in the domain
D
.

Let
\alpha_{\kappa}=\frac{8}{\kappa}-1
. For
y\geq 0
, we define
P_{y}\colon\mathbf{R}_{+}\to\mathbf{R}_{+}
by

(3.6)

P_{y}(x)=\begin{cases}y^{\alpha_{\kappa}-(2-d_{\kappa})}x^{2-d_{\kappa}}\quad&\text{if}\quad x\leq y,\\
x^{\alpha_{\kappa}}\quad&\text{if}\quad x\geq y.\end{cases}

Then it is shown in
[
46
, Theorem 1.1]
that if
z_{0},\ldots,z_{n}\in\mathrm{cl}(\mathbf{H})
are distinct and such that
z_{0}=0
,
y_{k}=\mathrm{Im}(z_{k})\geq 0
and
\ell_{k}=\mathrm{dist}(z_{k},\{z_{j}:1\leq j<k\})
for
1\leq k\leq n
, then we can find a constant
C_{n}<\infty
depending only on
\kappa
and
n\in\mathbf{N}
such that

(3.7)

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}[\mathrm{dist}(z_{k},\eta)\leq r_{k},\ 1\leq k\leq n]\leq C_{n}\prod_{k=1}^{n}\frac{P_{y_{k}}(r_{k}\wedge\ell_{k})}{P_{y_{k}}(\ell_{k})}\quad\text{for all}\quad r_{1},\ldots,r_{n}>0.

We fix
n\in\mathbf{N}
and
A\subseteq R
Borel such that
\mathrm{diam}(A)<\mathrm{dist}(R,\partial\mathbf{H})
. First we will find an upper bound for

(3.8)

\displaystyle r^{n(d_{\kappa}-2)}\mathbf{P}[\mathrm{dist}(z_{k},\eta)<r,\ 1\leq k\leq n]\quad\text{for}\quad z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\in A.

By (
3.7
), we have that (
3.8
) is bounded by

(3.9)

\displaystyle r^{n(d_{\kappa}-2)}C_{n}\prod_{k=1}^{n}\frac{P_{y_{k}}(r\wedge\ell_{k})}{P_{y_{k}}(\ell_{k})}.

Suppose that there exist
i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}
such that
r\leq\ell_{i_{j}}
for all
1\leq j\leq k
and
r>\ell_{j}
for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\setminus\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\}
. Then since

\frac{P_{y}(x_{1})}{P_{y}(x_{2})}\leq\frac{x_{1}^{2-d_{\kappa}}}{x_{2}^{2-d_{\kappa}}}\quad\text{for all}\quad y\geq 0\quad\text{and}\quad 0\leq x_{1}<x_{2}

we obtain that (
3.9
) is bounded by

\displaystyle C_{n}r^{n(d_{\kappa}-2)}\prod_{j=1}^{k}\frac{r^{2-d_{\kappa}}}{\ell_{i_{j}}^{2-d_{\kappa}}}\leq C_{n}r^{n(d_{\kappa}-2)}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{r^{2-d_{\kappa}}}{\ell_{i}^{2-d_{\kappa}}}=C_{n}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\ell_{i}^{d_{\kappa}-2}.

If either
r\leq\ell_{j}
for all
1\leq j\leq n
or
r>\ell_{j}
for all
1\leq j\leq n
, then a similar bound holds. Set

\displaystyle g(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})=\prod_{k=1}^{n}\ell_{k}^{d_{\kappa}-2}=\prod_{k=1}^{n}\min\{|z_{k}-z_{0}|,\ldots,|z_{k}-z_{k-1}|\}^{d_{\kappa}-2}.

Since
\mathrm{diam}(A)<\mathrm{dist}(R,\partial\mathbf{H})
, we obtain that
\ell_{k}=\min\{|z_{k}-z_{1}|,\ldots,|z_{k}-z_{k-1}|\}
. Hence for all
1\leq k\leq n
, we have that

\displaystyle\int_{A}\min\{|z_{k}-z_{0}|,\ldots,|z_{k}-z_{k-1}|\}^{d_{\kappa}-2}d\mathcal{L}_{2}(z_{k})

\displaystyle=\int_{A}\min\{|z_{k}-z_{1}|,\ldots,|z_{k}-z_{k-1}|\}^{d_{\kappa}-2}d\mathcal{L}_{2}(z_{k})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\int_{A}|z_{k}-z_{j}|^{d_{\kappa}-2}d\mathcal{L}_{2}(z_{k})

\displaystyle\leq n\int_{|z|\leq\mathrm{diam}(A)}|z|^{d_{\kappa}-2}d\mathcal{L}_{2}(z)\lesssim\mathrm{diam}(A)^{d_{\kappa}}

where the implicit constant depends only on
n
and
\kappa
. This implies that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}[\mathrm{Cont}_{d_{\kappa}}(\eta\cap A;r)^{n}]

\displaystyle=r^{n(d_{\kappa}-2)}\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\int_{A}{\bf 1}_{\mathrm{dist}(z,\eta)<r}d\mathcal{L}_{2}(z)\right)^{n}\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{A^{n}}r^{n(d_{\kappa}-2)}\mathbf{P}[\mathrm{dist}(z_{j},\eta)<r,\ 1\leq j\leq n]d\mathcal{L}_{2}(z_{1})\cdots d\mathcal{L}_{2}(z_{n})

\displaystyle\leq C_{n}\int_{A^{n}}g(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})d\mathcal{L}_{2}(z_{1})\cdots d\mathcal{L}_{2}(z_{n})

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathrm{diam}(A)^{nd_{\kappa}}

where the implicit constant depends only on
n
and
\kappa
. By applying Fatou’s lemma and using (
3.5
) we obtain that

(3.10)

\mathbf{E}[\mathrm{Cont}_{d_{\kappa}}(\eta\cap A)^{n}]\lesssim\mathrm{diam}(A)^{nd_{\kappa}}.

Note that a similar analysis gives that
\mathbf{E}[\mathrm{Cont}_{d_{\kappa}}(\eta\cap R)^{n}]<\infty
.

Next for
m\in\mathbf{Z}
we let
\mathcal{S}_{m}
be the set of dyadic squares contained in
R
with side length
2^{-m}
. Set
\mathcal{S}_{m}=\{S_{m,1},\dots,S_{m,n_{m}}\}
and note that
n_{m}\leq M2^{2m}
for all
m\in\mathbf{Z}
, where
M=\mathcal{L}_{2}(R)
. Also, we fix
\epsilon\in(0,1)
small and for
m\in\mathbf{Z}
,
1\leq j\leq n_{m}
, we set
X_{m,j}=\mathrm{Cont}_{d_{\kappa}}(\eta\cap S_{m,j})
and let
C_{m}
be the event that
X_{m,j}\geq 2^{-m(1-\epsilon)d_{\kappa}}
for some
j\in\{1,\dots,n_{m}\}
. Then combining (
3.10
) with
n_{m}\leq M2^{2m}
and
\mathrm{diam}(S_{m,j})<\mathrm{dist}(R,\partial\mathbf{H})
for all
m
sufficiently large, we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}[C_{m}]\lesssim 2^{m(2-\epsilon nd_{\kappa})}

for all
m
large enough, where the implicit constant depends only on
n
,
\kappa
and
R
. Hence if we take
n
large enough such that
2-\epsilon nd_{\kappa}<0
, we have that
\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\mathbf{P}[C_{m}]<\infty
and so there exists
m_{0}
large such that
\mathbf{P}[\cup_{m=m_{0}}^{\infty}C_{m}]<\frac{p}{2}
. Moreover there exists
N\in\mathbf{Z}_{-}
such that
\mathcal{S}_{m}=\emptyset
for all
m\leq N
and since
\mathbf{E}[\mathrm{Cont}_{d_{\kappa}}(\eta\cap R)^{n}]<\infty
, we can find a constant
\widetilde{C}<\infty
depending only on
n
,
\kappa
,
p
and
R
such that with probability at least
1-p
we have that
X_{m,j}\leq\widetilde{C}\mathrm{diam}(S_{m,j})^{d_{\kappa}-\epsilon d_{\kappa}}
for all
m\geq N
and all
1\leq j\leq n_{m}
. From now on, we assume that we are working on this event.

Finally, to complete the proof of the lemma, we let
A\subseteq K
be a Borel set and we fix
c_{1}>0
small (to be chosen independently of
A
). Let
m\in\mathbf{Z}
be the unique integer such that
2^{-m-1}<c_{1}\mathrm{diam}(A)\leq 2^{-m}
. Let also
S_{1},\dots,S_{k_{m}}
be the dyadic squares of side length
2^{-m}
intersecting
A
. Then
\mathrm{diam}(S_{j})=\sqrt{2}2^{-m}\leq c_{1}2^{3/2}\mathrm{diam}(A)
for all
1\leq j\leq k_{m}
. Also
A\subseteq B(x,\mathrm{diam}(A))
for a fixed
x\in A
and so
\bigcup_{j=1}^{k_{m}}S_{j}\subseteq B(x,(1+c_{1}2^{3/2})\mathrm{diam}(A))
. Thus we have that

\displaystyle k_{m}2^{-2m}=\mathcal{L}_{2}\!\left(\bigcup_{j=1}^{k_{m}}S_{j}\right)\leq\pi(1+c_{1}2^{3/2})^{2}\mathrm{diam}(A)^{2}\leq\pi(2^{3/2}+c_{1}^{-1})^{2}2^{-2m}

and so
k_{m}\leq\pi(2^{3/2}+c_{1}^{-1})^{2}
. Moreover by taking
c_{1}>0
small enough (depending only on
R
), we have that
S_{j}\subseteq R
and so
S_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{m}
for all
1\leq j\leq k_{m}
. Therefore we have that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cont}_{d_{\kappa}}(S_{j})\leq\widetilde{C}\mathrm{diam}(S_{j})^{d_{\kappa}(1-\epsilon)}\lesssim 2^{-md_{\kappa}(1-\epsilon)}

and so

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cont}_{d_{\kappa}}(\eta\cap A)\leq\sum_{j=1}^{k_{m}}\mathrm{Cont}_{d_{\kappa}}(S_{j})\lesssim 2^{-m(1-\epsilon)d_{\kappa}}\lesssim\mathrm{diam}(A)^{(1-\epsilon)d_{\kappa}}

where the implicit constants depend only on
n
,
\kappa
,
p
and
R
. Since
\epsilon\in(0,1)
was arbitrary, this completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

4.
First crossing lemmas

The purpose of this section is to collect a number of regularity estimates for the first crossing of an
{\rm SLE}_{4}
across an annulus. We will work with a conformal annulus rather than a Euclidean annulus as this will simplify the proofs in a number of places. We begin in Section
4.1
by describing the setup and notation. Next, in Section
4.2
we will show that the extremal length between the left and right sides of the conformal rectangle formed by cutting the conformal annulus with the first crossing of the
{\rm SLE}_{4}
with the left and right sides of the
{\rm SLE}_{4}
taken to the left and right sides of the rectangle is unlikely to be too large (Lemma
4.2
). We will also prove some additional estimates (Lemmas
4.3
,
4.4
) which control the regularity properties of the map which takes the cut annulus to the rectangle as well as the Lebesgue measure of a small neighborhood of the first crossing. Finally, in Section
4.3
(Lemma
4.5
) we will establish a version of Lemmas
3.1
,
3.2
which hold for the first crossing of the
{\rm SLE}_{4}
across an annulus.

4.1.
Setup and notation

Figure 1.
Illustration of the setup and notation described in Section
4.1
. Not shown is
A_{z,k}^{*}
, which is the component of
A_{z,k}\setminus\eta([0,\tau_{z,k}])
with
\partial^{\mathrm{in}}A_{z,k}
on its boundary.

See Figure
1
for an illustration of the setup and notation. Suppose that
\eta
is an
{\rm SLE}_{4}
in
\mathbf{H}
from
0
to
\infty
. For each
t\geq 0
we let
\mathcal{G}_{t}=\sigma(\eta(s):s\leq t)
and
\mathbf{H}_{t}=\mathbf{H}\setminus\eta([0,t])
. For each
k\in\mathbf{N}
we let
\tau_{z,k}^{*}=\inf\{t\geq 0:{\rm CR}(z,\mathbf{H}_{t})\leq e^{-4k}\}
. On
\tau_{z,k}^{*}<\infty
, we let
\psi_{z,k}:\mathbf{H}_{\tau_{z,k}^{*}}\to\mathbf{D}
be the unique conformal map satisfying
\psi_{z,k}(z)=0
and
\psi_{z,k}(\eta(\tau_{z,k}^{*}))=1
. We let
A_{z,k}=\psi_{z,k}^{-1}(B(0,e^{-1})\setminus\mathrm{cl}(B(0,e^{-2})))
,
\partial^{\mathrm{in}}A_{z,k}=\psi_{z,k}^{-1}(\partial B(0,e^{-2}))
,
\partial^{\mathrm{out}}A_{z,k}=\psi_{z,k}^{-1}(\partial B(0,e^{-1}))
, and
\tau_{z,k}=\inf\{t\geq 0:\eta(t)\in\partial^{\mathrm{in}}A_{z,k}\}
. We let
A_{z,k}^{*}
be the component of
A_{z,k}\setminus\eta([0,\tau_{z,k}])
with
\partial^{\mathrm{in}}A_{z,k}
on its boundary. Let
\sigma_{z,k}
be the largest time
t
before
\tau_{z,k}
that
\eta(t)\in\partial^{\mathrm{out}}A_{z,k}
. Let
H_{z,k}
be the unique positive real number so that there exists a conformal transformation
\varphi_{z,k}\colon A_{z,k}^{*}\to(0,1)\times(0,H_{z,k})
which takes
\eta^{\mathrm{R}}([\sigma_{z,k},\tau_{z,k}])
to
[0,H_{z,k}i]
and
\eta^{\mathrm{L}}([\sigma_{z,k},\tau_{z,k}])
to
1+[0,H_{z,k}i]
. Let also
(g_{t})
be the Loewner flow for
\eta
,
W
its Loewner driving function,
Z_{t}=g_{t}(z)-W_{t}=X_{t}+iY_{t}-W_{t}
and

\Delta_{t}=|g_{t}^{\prime}(z)|,\quad\Upsilon_{t}=\frac{Y_{t}}{|g_{t}^{\prime}(z)|},\quad\Theta_{t}=\arg Z_{t},\quad S_{t}=\sin\Theta_{t}.

4.2.
Extremal length of an annulus

Lemma 4.1
.

For every
p_{0}\in(0,1)
there exists
\delta_{0}>0
so that the following is true. For each
k
we let
\widetilde{\tau}_{z,k}=\inf\{t\geq\tau_{z,k-1}:S_{t}\leq\delta_{0}\}
. Then

\mathbf{P}[\tau_{z,k+1}<\infty\,|\,\mathcal{G}_{\widetilde{\tau}_{z,k}}]{\bf 1}_{\widetilde{\tau}_{z,k}<\infty}{\bf 1}_{\widetilde{\tau}_{z,k}\leq\tau_{z,k}}\leq p_{0}{\bf 1}_{\widetilde{\tau}_{z,k}<\infty}{\bf 1}_{\widetilde{\tau}_{z,k}\leq\tau_{z,k}}.

Proof.

We suppose that we are working on the event
\widetilde{\tau}_{z,k}<\infty
and
\widetilde{\tau}_{z,k}\leq\tau_{z,k}
. Let
\psi=(g_{\widetilde{\tau}_{z,k}}-W_{\widetilde{\tau}_{z,k}})/|Z_{\widetilde{\tau}_{z,k}}|
be the unique conformal transformation from
\mathbf{H}\setminus\eta([0,\widetilde{\tau}_{z,k}])
to
\mathbf{H}
which takes
\eta(\widetilde{\tau}_{z,k})
to
0
, fixes
\infty
and is normalized so the modulus of the image of
z
is equal to
1
. Note that
S_{\widetilde{\tau}_{z,k}}
is proportional to
\mathrm{Im}(\psi(z))
. Thus as
S_{\widetilde{\tau}_{z,k}}\leq\delta_{0}
we have that
\mathrm{Im}(\psi(z))\lesssim\delta_{0}
. Since
\widetilde{\eta}=\psi(\eta|_{[\widetilde{\tau}_{z,k},\infty)})
is an
{\rm SLE}_{4}
in
\mathbf{H}
from
0
to
\infty
and an
{\rm SLE}_{4}
a.s. does not intersect the domain boundary (except at its starting point) and is transient it follows that the probability that
\widetilde{\eta}
hits
\psi(B(z,e^{-4(k+1)}))
tends to
0
as
\delta_{0}\to 0
, which proves the result.
∎

Lemma 4.2
.

For every
p_{0}\in(0,1)
and
\delta_{0}>0
there exists
H_{0}>0
so that

\mathbf{P}\left[\tau_{z,k+1}<\infty,\ \inf_{\tau_{z,k}\leq t\leq\tau_{z,k+1}}S_{t}\geq\delta_{0},\ H_{z,k+1}\leq H_{0}\,|\,\mathcal{G}_{\tau_{z,k}}\right]{\bf 1}_{\tau_{z,k}<\infty}\leq p_{0}{\bf 1}_{\tau_{z,k}<\infty}.

We will first collect some preliminary facts before giving the proof of Lemma
4.2
. For each
r\in\mathbf{R}
we let

a=\frac{2}{\kappa},\quad\lambda=\frac{r^{2}}{2a}+r\left(1-\frac{1}{2a}\right),\quad\xi=\frac{r^{2}}{4a}.

Recall from
[
18
, Section 6.1]
(see also
[
51
]
) that

M_{t}=S_{t}^{-r}\Upsilon_{t}^{\xi+r}\Delta_{t}^{\lambda+r}

is a continuous local martingale for
{\rm SLE}_{4}
. Making the choice

r=1-\frac{8}{\kappa}=-1

we see that

M_{t}=S_{t}\Upsilon_{t}^{-1/2}

is a continuous local martingale for
{\rm SLE}_{4}
. Moreover, it follows from
[
51
, Theorem 6]
that if we weight the law of an
{\rm SLE}_{4}
by
M_{t}
then the resulting process is an
{\rm SLE}_{4}(-4)
up to time
t
. Recall from
[
51
, Theorem 3]
that a chordal
{\rm SLE}_{4}(-4)
process has the same law as a radial
{\rm SLE}_{4}(2)
process (up to time change). Note that
\Upsilon_{t}={\rm CR}(z,\mathbf{H}_{t})/2
.

Proof of Lemma
4.2
.

Fix
p_{0}\in(0,1)
,
\delta_{0}>0
, and
H_{0}>0
. Let
E_{z,k+1}
be the event that
\tau_{z,k+1}<\infty
,
\inf_{\tau_{z,k}\leq t\leq\tau_{z,k+1}}S_{t}\geq\delta_{0}
, and
H_{z,k+1}\leq H_{0}
. We have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}[E_{z,k+1}\,|\,\mathcal{G}_{\tau_{z,k}}]{\bf 1}_{\tau_{z,k}<\infty}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\frac{M_{\tau_{z,k+1}}}{M_{\tau_{z,k}}}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{M_{\tau_{z,k+1}}}{M_{\tau_{z,k}}}\right){\bf 1}_{E_{z,k+1}}\,|\,\mathcal{G}_{\tau_{z,k}}\right]{\bf 1}_{\tau_{z,k}<\infty}

\displaystyle=S_{\tau_{z,k}}\mathbf{E}_{z,k}^{*}\left[S_{\tau_{z,k+1}}^{-1}\left(\frac{\Upsilon_{\tau_{z,k+1}}}{\Upsilon_{\tau_{z,k}}}\right)^{1/2}{\bf 1}_{E_{z,k+1}}\right]{\bf 1}_{\tau_{z,k}<\infty}

where
\mathbf{P}_{z,k}^{*}
denotes the law of a radial
{\rm SLE}_{4}(2)
process in
\mathbf{H}\setminus\eta([0,\tau_{z,k}])
from
\eta(\tau_{z,k})
to
z
, until time
\tau_{z,k+1}
and with force point located at
\infty
, and
\mathbf{E}_{z,k}^{*}
denotes the expectation with respect to
\mathbf{P}_{z,k}^{*}
. Note that
\mathbf{P}_{z,k}^{*}[\tau_{z,k+1}<\infty]=1
. We further note that
\Upsilon_{\tau_{z,k+1}}/\Upsilon_{\tau_{z,k}}\leq 1
and
S_{\tau_{z,k+1}}^{-1}\leq\delta_{0}^{-1}
on
E_{z,k+1}
. It therefore suffices to bound

\displaystyle S_{\tau_{z,k}}\delta_{0}^{-1}\mathbf{P}_{z,k}^{*}[E_{z,k+1}]{\bf 1}_{\tau_{z,k}<\infty}\leq\delta_{0}^{-1}\mathbf{P}_{z,k}^{*}[E_{z,k+1}]{\bf 1}_{\tau_{z,k}<\infty}.

On
\tau_{z,k}<\infty
, we have that

\displaystyle 4k+2-\log 4

\displaystyle=-\log{\rm CR}(z,\mathbf{H}_{\tau_{z,k}^{*}})-\log(4e^{-2})

\displaystyle\leq-\log{\rm CR}(z,\mathbf{H}_{\tau_{z,k}})\quad\text{(by Koebe-$1/4$)}

\displaystyle\leq-\log{\rm CR}(z,\mathbf{H}_{\tau_{z,k}^{*}})-\log(e^{-2}/4)\quad\text{(by Koebe-$1/4$)}

\displaystyle=4k+2+\log 4.

This implies that

\log{\rm CR}(z,\mathbf{H}_{\tau_{z,k}})-\log{\rm CR}(z,\mathbf{H}_{\tau_{z,k+1}^{*}})\geq 1/2.

Thus if we let
\widehat{\tau}_{z,k}=\inf\{t\geq 0:{\rm CR}(z,\mathbf{H}_{t})\leq e^{-4k-1/2}\}
we have that
\tau_{z,k}\leq\widehat{\tau}_{z,k}\leq\tau_{z,k+1}^{*}
. Let
\theta_{z,k+1}\in[0,2\pi)
be such that
\psi_{z,k+1}(\infty)=e^{i\theta_{z,k+1}}
. Note that under
\mathbf{P}^{*}_{z,k}
the curve
\widehat{\eta}=\psi_{z,k}(\eta|_{[\tau_{z,k}^{*},\tau_{z})})
has the law of a radial
{\rm SLE}_{4}(2)
in
\mathbf{D}
from
1
to
0
with the force point located at
\psi_{z,k}(\infty)
, where
\tau_{z}
is the first time that
\eta
hits
z
. Let
(\widehat{W},\widehat{O})
be the driving pair for
\widehat{\eta}
and set
\widehat{\theta}_{t}=\arg(\widehat{O}_{t})-\arg(\widehat{W}_{t})
. We parameterize
\widehat{\eta}
by log conformal radius as seen from
0
. Let also
\widehat{\psi}_{z,k}
be the unique conformal transformation mapping
\mathbf{H}_{\widehat{\tau}_{z,k}}
onto
\mathbf{D}
such that
\widehat{\psi}_{z,k}(z)=0
and
\widehat{\psi}_{z,k}(\eta(\widehat{\tau}_{z,k}))=1
and let
\widehat{\theta}_{z,k}\in[0,2\pi)
be such that
\widehat{\psi}_{z,k}(\infty)=e^{i\widehat{\theta}_{z,k}}
. By the definitions of
\widehat{\tau}_{z,k}
and
\tau_{z,k+1}^{*}
and the Markov property of
\widehat{\theta}
under
\mathbf{P}^{*}_{z,k}
it follows that the conditional law of
\theta_{z,k+1}
given
\widehat{\theta}_{z,k}
is that of
\widehat{\theta}_{2}
. Let
f
be the density of the law of
\theta_{z,k+1}
under
\mathbf{P}^{*}_{z,k}
with respect to Lebesgue measure. By
[
27
, Proposition 4.4]
, it follows that there exists a universal constant
C<\infty
such that

(4.1)

\displaystyle C^{-1}\sin(y/2)^{2}\leq f(y)\leq C\sin(y/2)^{2}\quad\text{for all}\quad y\in(0,2\pi).

For each
\theta\in(0,2\pi)
we let
\mathbf{P}_{\theta}^{*}
denote the law of a radial
{\rm SLE}_{4}(2)
process
\widetilde{\eta}
in
\mathbf{D}
from
1
to
0
where the force point at time
0
is located at
e^{i\theta}
. Let
\widetilde{\tau}=\inf\{t\geq 0:\widetilde{\eta}(t)\in\partial B(0,e^{-2})\}
, let
\widetilde{\sigma}
be the largest time
t
before
\widetilde{\tau}
that
\widetilde{\eta}(t)\in\partial B(0,e^{-1})
, and let
\widetilde{A}^{*}
be the component of
B(0,e^{-1})\setminus\mathrm{cl}(B(0,e^{-2}))
with
\partial B(0,e^{-2})
on its boundary. Finally, let
\widetilde{H}>0
be the unique positive real number so that there exists a conformal map
\widetilde{\varphi}\colon\widetilde{A}^{*}\to(0,1)\times(0,\widetilde{H})
which takes the right (resp. left) side of
\widetilde{\eta}([\widetilde{\sigma},\widetilde{\tau}])
to
[0,\widetilde{H}i]
(resp.
1+[0,\widetilde{H}i]
). Then it suffices to show that

\mathbf{E}_{z,k}^{*}[\mathbf{P}_{\theta_{z,k+1}}^{*}[\widetilde{H}\leq H_{0}]]{\bf 1}_{\tau_{z,k}<\infty}\to 0\quad\text{uniformly as}\quad H_{0}\to 0.

To prove the above, we note that (
4.1
) implies that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}^{*}_{z,k}[\mathbf{P}_{\theta_{z,k+1}}^{*}[\widetilde{H}\leq H_{0}]]=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathbf{P}_{\theta}^{*}[\widetilde{H}\leq H_{0}]f(\theta)d\theta\leq C\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathbf{P}_{\theta}^{*}[\widetilde{H}\leq H_{0}]\sin(\theta/2)^{2}d\theta.

Also
\widetilde{H}
is a.s. positive as a radial
{\rm SLE}_{4}(2)
is a.s. a simple curve which implies that
\mathbf{P}_{\theta}^{*}[\widetilde{H}\leq H_{0}]\to 0
as
H_{0}\to 0
. The claim then follows by applying the dominated convergence theorem. This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 4.3
.

For every
